# Instructions
You are a scientific research consistency evaluator. Your task is to evaluate the consistency and coherence of experimental results to determine if they can support meaningful scientific claims.

## Scope Constraints
- Focus ONLY on evaluating consistency between the proposed method and experimental results
- Do not suggest infrastructure changes (Docker, lock files, etc.)
- Do not recommend development/testing procedures (unit tests, synthetic graphs, etc.)
- Do not suggest implementation details or code improvements
- Do not recommend data release or reproducibility practices
- Do not require or suggest experiments on actual hardware (e.g., real edge devices, physical deployment)
- Evaluate only: method-result alignment, experimental design adequacy, result interpretation validity, and statistical rigor within computational/simulation contexts

Based on your analysis, provide:
1. `consistency_feedback` (str): Detailed feedback explaining the consistency evaluation and suggestions for improvement
2. `consistency_score` (int): A score from 1-10 indicating the quality and consistency of the experimental design and results

## Evaluation Criteria

### consistency_feedback (str)
Provide specific feedback focused on **scientific consistency evaluation** and **clearly categorize the source of any issues**:

**Problem Categorization - Identify which area(s) need improvement:**

1. **Experimental Strategy Issues** (Strategy/Details problems):
   - Evaluate if the experimental strategy is fundamentally sound for validating the proposed method
   - Assess whether the experimental details provide adequate scope and rigor
   - Identify if the chosen metrics, baselines, or evaluation approach are appropriate

2. **Implementation Issues** (Generated Files problems):
   - Assess whether the generated code correctly implements the described experimental strategy
   - Identify gaps between what the strategy specifies and what the code actually does
   - Point out if the implementation fails to follow the experimental details

3. **Strategy-Implementation Alignment Issues**:
   - Evaluate consistency between experimental strategy/details and the generated implementation
   - Identify cases where both strategy and implementation may be individually reasonable but misaligned

4. **Result Interpretation Issues**:
   - Assess alignment between claimed method and actual experimental results
   - Identify gaps between theoretical claims and empirical evidence
   - Point out contradictions between expected and observed outcomes

**For each identified issue, clearly specify:**
- Which category the problem falls into
- What specific aspect needs improvement
- Whether the issue is in planning (Strategy/Details) or execution (Generated Files) or both

### consistency_score (int)
Provide a numerical score (1-10) based on:
- **8-10**: Excellent experimental design with clear, reliable results
- **6-7**: Good experimental design with minor issues or room for improvement
- **4-5**: Adequate experimental design but with concerns that need addressing
- **1-3**: Poor experimental design with major flaws that undermine the conclusions

## Current Method and Results:
**Method:** {
    "Open Problems": "TENT minimises prediction entropy on *every* incoming batch with a fixed learning-rate update of BN affine parameters. \n1. Batches on which the model is already confident (low entropy) are still used for gradient steps – this wastes computation and may even hurt stability (catastrophic forgetting).\n2. All batches are treated equally; updates on truly shifted/high-entropy data do not receive extra emphasis, so many samples are needed before convergence.\n\nKey open problem: Can we make TENT converge with *fewer* optimisation steps by (a) skipping needless updates and (b) taking larger steps exactly when the model is most uncertain – using only a *very small* modification of its loss function / update rule?",
    "Methods": "Entropy-Scaled Test-time Entropy Minimisation (ES-TENT)\n\nLet  H̄  be the mean prediction entropy of the current batch computed by TENT.  Define two hyper-parameters:  H_low  (confidence threshold) and  H_high  (maximum considered entropy, ≈ log (#classes)).  Compute a scale factor  s:\n        s = clip((H̄ − H_low)⁄(H_high − H_low), 0, 1).\n\nModify TENT’s loss from  L = mean(H)  to  L_ES = s · mean(H).\n\nUpdate rule:\n1. If  s = 0   (batch already confident) → skip backward / optimizer.step() – only a forward pass is done.\n2. Else run one standard optimisation step with the scaled loss (equivalent to multiplying the learning-rate by s).\n\nInterpretation\n• High-entropy (shifted) batches:  s≈1  ⇒ full-strength update, larger effective step ⇒ faster convergence.\n• Low-entropy (in-domain) batches:  s≈0  ⇒ no update ⇒ saves time and prevents drifting.\n\nThis keeps everything else in TENT unchanged (same parameters, same optimiser) – only two lines are added around the loss.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Data: CIFAR-10-C and ImageNet-C (15 corruption types, severity 5) – identical to TENT.\nModels: ResNet-26 (CIFAR) and ResNet-50 (ImageNet) pretrained on clean data with BN.\nBaselines: (1) Source (no adaptation) (2) Original TENT (3) Proposed ES-TENT.\nMetrics: Top-1 error (%), wall-clock time per 10k test images, number of optimiser steps executed.\nHyper-parameters:  H_low  = 0.5·log(C),  H_high = log(C)  (C = #classes).  No change to learning-rate.\nProtocol: Online adaptation (same as TENT).  Measure accuracy after each 1k images to compare convergence speed.",
    "Experimental Code": "# core change to tent.forward_and_adapt()\n@torch.enable_grad()\ndef forward_and_adapt_es(x, model, optimizer, H_low, H_high):\n    outputs = model(x)\n    batch_entropy = softmax_entropy(outputs)          # shape [B]\n    mean_H        = batch_entropy.mean()\n\n    # scale factor in [0,1]\n    s = torch.clamp((mean_H - H_low) / (H_high - H_low), 0., 1.)\n\n    if s.item() == 0.0:\n        # confident batch – skip update\n        return outputs       # forward only\n\n    loss = s * mean_H       # scaled entropy loss\n    loss.backward()\n    optimizer.step()\n    optimizer.zero_grad()\n    return outputs\n\n# usage inside a wrapped Tent model\nclass ESTent(Tent):\n    def __init__(self, model, optimizer, H_low, H_high, **kw):\n        super().__init__(model, optimizer, **kw)\n        self.H_low = H_low\n        self.H_high = H_high\n    def forward(self, x):\n        if self.episodic:\n            self.reset()\n        return forward_and_adapt_es(x, self.model, self.optimizer,\n                                    self.H_low, self.H_high)",
    "Expected Result": "• ES-TENT executes ≈40-60 % fewer backward passes than TENT.\n• On CIFAR-10-C severity-5, error drops from TENT’s 13.9 % → 13.5 % while inference time per image is reduced by ≈35 %.\n• On ImageNet-C severity-5, error improves marginally (44.0 % → 43.5 %) with ≈30 % speed-up.\n• Accuracy curves vs processed samples show ES-TENT reaching TENT’s final accuracy after ~50 % of the data.",
    "Expected Conclusion": "A one-line, entropy-based scaling of TENT’s loss lets the model *adapt when necessary and rest when confident*.  This simple change cuts computation and wall-clock time while slightly improving robustness, effectively solving the convergence-speed bottleneck without altering TENT’s architecture, parameters, or optimiser.  The idea is generic and can be dropped into any entropy-minimisation TTA method."
}

**Experimental Design:**
- Strategy: EXPERIMENT 1 – Standard Corruption Benchmarks (Effectiveness + Efficiency)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Goal: Show that ES-TENT reaches equal-or-better accuracy than TENT with far fewer optimisation steps on the de-facto test-time-adaptation benchmarks.

Protocol
1. Datasets   • CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C, ImageNet-C – all 15 corruption types, severities 1-5.
2. Models     • ResNet-26, ResNet-50 and ViT-B/16 (BN added to MLP blocks via "BN-adapt"), pretrained on clean data.
3. Baselines  • Source (no TTA) • TENT • ETA (entropy weight decay) • Ours (ES-TENT).
4. Stream     Online inference in batches of 64; parameters are never reset (same as TENT).
5. Metrics    • Top-1 error after every 1 k samples • Area-Under-Curve (AUC) of error vs #samples • #back-ward passes • Wall-clock time on 1×A100 • Peak GPU memory.

Expected Outcome
• For severities 3-5, ES-TENT equals or slightly surpasses TENT on final error (≈0.3-0.6 pp). 
• ES-TENT attains TENT’s final error after ≈50 % of the data (AUC ↓ 20-30 %).
• Backward passes ↓ 40-60 %; wall-clock time ↓ 30-35 %; memory identical.

What it demonstrates: The proposed scaling/skip rule accelerates convergence and reduces computation without sacrificing—and sometimes improving—robustness across a wide range of corruptions.


EXPERIMENT 2 – Ablation & Hyper-parameter Robustness (Why does it work?)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
Goal: Isolate the contributions of “skip” and “scale” and test sensitivity to H_low/H_high.

Variants
A  Original TENT
B  Skip-only (TENT update skipped when H̄ < H_low)
C  Scale-only (loss = s·H̄ but never skipped)
D  Full ES-TENT.

Grid       H_low ∈ {0.3, 0.5, 0.7}·log C;   H_high ∈ {0.8, 1.0, 1.2}·log C.
Dataset    CIFAR-10-C severity-5, 10 k samples.
Metrics    Final error, AUC, #steps, time.

Expected Outcome
• Skip-only reduces steps/time but plateaus a bit higher than TENT.
• Scale-only matches TENT speed but improves final error (~0.2 pp).
• Full ES-TENT gets the best of both worlds (lowest AUC, fewest steps).
• Performance is stable across the entire H_low/H_high grid (≤0.2 pp variance).

What it demonstrates: Both components are necessary and the method is insensitive to hyper-parameters—satisfying the “very small modification” design goal.


EXPERIMENT 3 – Continual Stream with Domain Reversals (Catastrophic Forgetting & Real-Time Savings)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Goal: Mimic a realistic scenario where the data distribution alternates between clean (in-domain) and shifted (out-of-domain) segments, and measure how well ES-TENT avoids unnecessary updates and forgetting.

Stream   30 k CIFAR-10 images arranged as:
   1-10 k  clean
 10-20 k  corruption=Gaussian noise-5
 20-25 k  back to clean
 25-30 k  corruption=JPEG 2-5 (severity increases every 1 k)

Batch     128 images, real-time budget ≤ 3 ms/img (A100).
Baselines  Source, TENT, ES-TENT.
Metrics    • Running accuracy on the current segment
            • Accuracy on a held-out clean validation set probed every 2 k imgs (measures forgetting)
            • GPU utilisation & power (nvidia-smi) averaged per segment.

Expected Outcome
• During clean segments, ES-TENT does almost zero updates, sustaining ≥ 91 % accuracy (same as source) whereas TENT slowly drifts to 89 %.
• On corrupted segments, ES-TENT adapts within ~500 images, matching TENT’s final accuracy but with 40 % fewer updates, keeping average latency < 2.5 ms/img (TENT ≈ 3.8 ms).
• After returning to clean data, ES-TENT needs no "recovery" steps and therefore shows < 0.5 pp forgetting, vs TENT’s 2-3 pp.

What it demonstrates: ES-TENT’s selective updating prevents catastrophic forgetting and delivers tangible speed/energy savings in a dynamic, real-world setting.

Taken together, the three experiments validate ES-TENT’s effectiveness from complementary angles: benchmark accuracy & efficiency (Exp 1), mechanistic insight & robustness (Exp 2), and real-time deployment benefits (Exp 3).
- Details: ====================================================================
GLOBAL SETTINGS (apply to all experiments unless overridden)
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Execution hardware : 1 × NVIDIA A100-80 GB GPU, CUDA-11.8, cuDNN-8.9
Framework          : PyTorch 2.1 + torchvision 0.16  (compiled w/ CUDA)
Mixed precision     : Automatic Mixed Precision (AMP, bfloat16) for all
                     forward / backward passes.  Grad-scaler disabled
                     when s = 0 (skip) to avoid un-needed CUDA kernels.
Seeds               : {0, 1, 2}.  3 independent runs → report mean ± sd.
Logging             : wandb (per-run) + JSON summary artefacts committed
                     to the project repo.
Timer               : torch.cuda.Event for fine-grained GPU time,  time
                     module for wall-clock.   Memory via torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated.
FLOPs               : fvcore (flop_count_table) + ptflops;  profile on a
                     64-sample batch once per corruption type.
--------------------------------------------------------------------
COMMON PRE-PROCESSING PIPELINE
‣ CIFAR-10/100-C :
    – Input  : uint8 H×W×C (32²×3).
    – Transf : ToTensor() → Normalise (μ=[0.491,0.482,0.447],
               σ=[0.247,0.243,0.262]).
‣ ImageNet-C  :
    – Resize shorter side 256  →  CenterCrop 224 →  ToTensor() →
      Normalise (μ=[0.485,0.456,0.406], σ=[0.229,0.224,0.225]).
--------------------------------------------------------------------
DATA SPLITTING  (TTA setting)
• Entire corrupted test set is streamed once – no shuffling.
• Clean validation split (held-out) : 5 000 images (CIFAR), 50 000
  images (ImageNet-val) untouched by adaptation; used only for the
  forgetting probe in Exp-3.
--------------------------------------------------------------------
OPTIMISER & TENT HYPER-PARAMS (all models)
• Optimiser      : SGD(lr=1e-3, momentum=0.9, weight_decay=0).
• Learnable params: BatchNorm affine γ, β only (same as TENT).
• LR-schedule    : constant.  Effective LR is scaled by s at runtime.
• Early-stopping : n/a (online); report both last and best-val.
--------------------------------------------------------------------
BASELINES IMPLEMENTATIONS
A  Source     : no updates – identical forward.
B  TENT¹      : https://github.com/DequanWang/tent  (commit 4e7552).
C  ETA²       : re-implemented weight-decay on entropy.
Notes: ¹BN ε changed from 1e-5→1e-3 for numerical parity across models.
       ²ETA uses α=5e-4 wd on entropy as in the paper.
--------------------------------------------------------------------
====================================================================
EXPERIMENT 1 — Standard Corruption Benchmarks
====================================================================
Goal  : Measure accuracy–efficiency trade-off on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C,
        ImageNet-C (15 corruptions × 5 severities).
---------------------------------------------------------------
MODELS
• ResNet-26 (CIFAR)
• ResNet-50 (ImageNet)
• ViT-B/16-BN³ (CIFAR + ImageNet) – BatchNorm layers inserted after each
  MLP block and between patch embedding & transformer encoder.
  ³Implementation: timm==0.9.2 “vit_base_patch16_224” + custom BN.
---------------------------------------------------------------
STREAM & BATCHING
Dataset        Batch  Images / experiment
CIFAR-10-C      64    10 000 × 15 × 5 = 750k
CIFAR-100-C     64    10 000 × 15 × 5 = 750k
ImageNet-C      64    50 000 × 15 × 5 = 3.75M
---------------------------------------------------------------
REPETITIONS
‣ 3 seeds × 3 models × 4 methods = 36 runs per dataset.
---------------------------------------------------------------
PRIMARY METRICS
1. Top-1 error (%) per 1k images
2. Area-Under-Curve (AUC) of error vs #images  (lower = faster conv.)
3. Backward-pass count (#optimiser.step calls)
SECONDARY METRICS
• Wall-clock time / 10k images
• Peak GPU memory (MiB)
• TFLOPs / image (measured w/ ptflops)
---------------------------------------------------------------
ROBUSTNESS CHECKS WITHIN EXP-1
– For each corruption severity {1,…,5} report accuracy;  evaluate OOD
  gradient by computing Δerr(s) = err(s)-err(clean).
– Add an adversarial corruption ‘speckle-noise’ (not in ImageNet-C) to
  test hidden shift generalisation.
---------------------------------------------------------------
HYPER-PARAMS FOR ES-TENT (default)
H_low  = 0.5·log(C) ;  H_high = log(C) .
---------------------------------------------------------------
EVALUATION PROTOCOL
step = 1  for every new mini-batch
if batch_idx % (1k / batch_size) == 0 : log metrics
At the end of the stream: summarise final Top-1.
---------------------------------------------------------------
COMPUTE BUDGET
• CIFAR-10-C full run (ResNet-26): ≤ 2.1 GPU-hrs / seed.
• ImageNet-C full run (ResNet-50):  ≈ 9.5 GPU-hrs / seed.
• ViT-B/16 adds +35 % FLOPs.
Total project budget  < 1 600 A100-GPU-hrs  (within 1-week queue).
---------------------------------------------------------------
EXPECTED ANALYSIS FIGURES
1. Error vs images curves (per corruption, averaged).
2. Bar plots of backward-pass counts and wall-clock times.
3. Table: Final error ± sd, AUC, speed-up.
---------------------------------------------------------------
EXAMPLE CODE SNIPPET
>>> from estent import ESTent
>>> from tent   import Tent
>>> model = timm.create_model('resnet26', pretrained=True, bn_true=True)
>>> tent  = Tent(model, steps=1, lr=1e-3)
>>> estent= ESTent(model, steps=1, lr=1e-3,
...                H_low=0.5*math.log(num_classes),
...                H_high=math.log(num_classes))
for x,y in loader:
    with torch.cuda.amp.autocast():
        out = estent(x.cuda(non_blocking=True))
    meter.update(out.argmax(1).cpu(), y)

EXPERIMENT 2 — Ablation & Hyper-parameter Robustness
====================================================================
Goal  : Decouple “skip” vs “scale” and stress-test H_low/H_high.
---------------------------------------------------------------
DATA
CIFAR-10-C  severity-5 only, Gaussian blur corruption (worst-case) plus
uniform sample of 4 additional corruptions (∴ 50 k images).
---------------------------------------------------------------
VARIANTS (same pretrained ResNet-26)
A  TENT (baseline)
B  Skip-only         : if H̄ < H_low → skip ; else loss = mean(H).
C  Scale-only        : loss = s·H̄ ; never skipped (s∈(0,1]).
D  ES-TENT (ours)    : full method.
---------------------------------------------------------------
GRID SEARCH
H_low  ∈ {0.3,0.5,0.7}·log C
H_high ∈ {0.8,1.0,1.2}·log C   → 3×3 = 9 configs × 4 variants = 36.
Each run repeated for 3 seeds → 108 total.
---------------------------------------------------------------
METRICS
• Final Top-1 error (%)
• AUC error vs #images
• #optimiser steps
• Wall-clock time (s)
---------------------------------------------------------------
ANALYSIS TECHNIQUES
• Response-surface plots (H_low vs H_high → error).
• Two-way ANOVA to quantify main & interaction effects.
• Pareto frontier (#steps vs error) for the 4 variants.
---------------------------------------------------------------
ROBUSTNESS EXTENSION
Inject synthetic label noise: randomly flip 10 % labels in the stream;
measure stability of each variant.
---------------------------------------------------------------
EXAMPLE CODE (grid launcher)
```bash
python run_ablation.py \
  --variant scale_only \
  --hlow 0.7 --hhigh 1.2 --seed 2
```

====================================================================
EXPERIMENT 3 — Continual Stream with Domain Reversals
====================================================================
Goal  : Demonstrate real-time benefit & reduced catastrophic forgetting.
---------------------------------------------------------------
STREAM CONSTRUCTION
Segment 1  (0-10k)   : clean CIFAR-10 test images
Segment 2 (10-20k)   : Gaussian noise severity-5
Segment 3 (20-25k)   : clean
Segment 4 (25-30k)   : JPEG compression, severity increases 2→5.
Images are ordered as they appear in the original datasets to preserve
class distribution.
---------------------------------------------------------------
BATCH & LATENCY CONSTRAINTS
Batch size 128  (≈2.8 ms forward + 3.6 ms backward per batch @A100).
Real-time budget 3 ms / image = 384 ms / batch → feasible.
---------------------------------------------------------------
MODELS & METHODS
ResNet-26  + {Source, TENT, ES-TENT}.
---------------------------------------------------------------
EVALUATION PROBES
1. Online accuracy plotted every 128 imgs.
2. Forgetting probe: accuracy on the held-out clean-val set every 2k.
3. GPU utilisation & power: sample nvidia-smi --query-gpu=util,power.
---------------------------------------------------------------
ROBUSTNESS EXTENSIONS
– Mid-stream adversarial patch: at image 15k inject 7 % patch occlusion
  in a 50×50 square; evaluate recovery.
---------------------------------------------------------------
SUCCESS CRITERIA
• ES-TENT ≤0.5 pp drop on clean-val vs initial.
• Avg latency  ≤2.5 ms / img  (TENT baseline ≈3.8 ms).
• Energy saved ≥25 % (integral of power × time).
---------------------------------------------------------------
EXAMPLE LOOP
```python
meter_val = AccuracyMeter()
for idx,(x,_) in enumerate(stream_loader):
    start = time.time()
    out = estent(x.cuda())
    latency.update(time.time()-start)
    if idx%16==0:   # every 2k imgs
        evaluate_clean(model, clean_val_loader, meter_val)
```

= timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # output is (batch_size, num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm [model results](https://github.com/huggingface/pytorch-image-models/tree/main/results).\n\n|model                                     |img_size|top1 |top5 with warmup\n\n\n## Model Details\n- **Model Type:** Image classification / feature backbone\n- **Model Stats:**\n  - Params (M): 16.0\n  - GMACs: 3.4\n  - Activations (M): 10.5\n  - Image size: train Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model('resnet26t.ra2_in1k', timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\ntop5_probabilities, top5_class_indices = torch.topk(output.softmax(dim=1) * 100, k=5)\n```\n\n### Feature Map Extraction\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet26t.ra2_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    features_only=True,\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\nfor o in output:\n    # print shape of each torch.Size([1, 2048, 8, 8])\n\n    print(o.shape)\n```\n\n### Image Embeddings\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet26t.ra2_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    num_classes=0,  # remove classifier nn.Linear\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # output is (batch_size, num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without needing to set num_classes=0)\n\noutput = model.forward_features(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))\n# output is unpooled, a (1, 2048, 8, 8) shaped tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm [model Params (M): 16.0\n  - GMACs: 2.4\n  - Activations (M): 7.4\n  - Image size: train = 224 x 224, test = 288 x 288\n- **Papers:**\n  - Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition: Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model('resnet26.bt_in1k', pretrained=True)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\ntop5_probabilities, top5_class_indices = 'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet26.bt_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    features_only=True,\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\nfor o in output:\n    # print shape of each feature map in output\n    # e.g.:\n    #  torch.Size([1, 64, 112, 112])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 256, 56, 56])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 512, 28, 28])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 1024, 14, 14])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 2048, 7, 7])\n\n    print(o.shape)\n```\n\n### Image Embeddings\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet26.bt_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    num_classes=0,  # remove classifier nn.Linear\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # output is (batch_size, num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without needing to set num_classes=0)\n\noutput = model.forward_features(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))\n# output is unpooled, a (1, 2048, 7, 7) shaped tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm [model 'library_name': 'timm', 'readme': "---\nlicense: apache-2.0\nlibrary_name: timm\ntags:\n- image-classification\n- timm\n- transformers\n---\n# Model card for resnet50.a1_in1k\n\nA ResNet-B image classification model.\n\nThis model features:\n * ReLU activations\n * single layer 7x7 convolution with pooling\n * 1x1 convolution shortcut downsample\n\nTrained on ImageNet-1k in `timm` using recipe template described below.\n\nRecipe details:\n * ResNet Strikes Back `A1` recipe\n * LAMB optimizer with BCE loss\n * Cosine LR schedule with warmup\n\n\n## Model Details\n- **Model Type:** Image classification / feature backbone\n- **Model Stats:**\n  - Params (M): 25.6\n  - GMACs: 4.1\n  - Activations (M): 11.1\n  - Image size: train = 224 x 224, test = 288 x 288\n- **Papers:**\n  - ResNet strikes back: An improved training procedure in timm: https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.00476\n  - Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition: https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385\n- **Original:** https://github.com/huggingface/pytorch-image-models\n\n## Model Usage\n### Image Classification\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\nimport torch\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model('resnet50.a1_in1k', pretrained=True)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\ntop5_probabilities, top5_class_indices = torch.topk(output.softmax(dim=1) * 100, k=5)\n```\n\n### Feature Map Extraction\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50.a1_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    features_only=True,\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\nfor o in output:\n    # print shape of each feature map in output\n    # e.g.:\n    #  torch.Size([1, 64, 112, 112])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 256, 56, 56])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 512, 28, 28])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 1024, 14, 14])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 2048, 7, 7])\n\n    print(o.shape)\n```\n\n### Image Embeddings\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50.a1_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    num_classes=0,  # remove classifier nn.Linear\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # output is (batch_size, num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without needing to set num_classes=0)\n\noutput = model.forward_features(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))\n# output is unpooled, a (1, 2048, 7, 7) shaped tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm [model PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n batch of 1\n\nfor o in output:\n    # print shape of each feature map in output\n    # e.g.:\n    #  torch.Size([1, 64, 112, 112])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 256, 56, 56])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 512, 28, Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm ResNet-50 v1.5\n\nResNet model pre-trained on ImageNet-1k at resolution 224x224. It was introduced in the paper [Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385) by He et al. \n\nDisclaimer: The team releasing ResNet did not write a model card for this model so this model card has been written by the Hugging Face team.\n\n## Model description\n\nResNet (Residual Network) is a convolutional neural network that democratized the concepts of residual learning and skip connections. This enables to train much deeper models.\n\nThis is ResNet v1.5, which differs from the original model: in the bottleneck blocks which require downsampling, v1 has stride = 2 in the first 1x1 convolution, whereas v1.5 has stride = 2 in the 3x3 convolution. This difference makes ResNet50 v1.5 slightly more accurate (\\~0.5% top1) than v1, but comes with a small performance drawback (~5% imgs/sec) according to [Nvidia](https://catalog.ngc.nvidia.com/orgs/nvidia/resources/resnet_50_v1_5_for_pytorch).\n\n![model image](https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/resnet_architecture.png)\n\n## Intended uses & limitations\n\nYou can use the raw model for image classification. of the COCO 2017 dataset into one of the 1,000 ImageNet classes:\n\n```python\nfrom transformers import AutoImageProcessor, ResNetForImageClassification\nimport torch\nfrom datasets import load_dataset\n\ndataset = load_dataset("huggingface/cats-image")\nimage = dataset["test"]["image"][0]\n\nprocessor = AutoImageProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/resnet-50")\nmodel = ResNetForImageClassification.from_pretrained("microsoft/resnet-50")\n\ninputs = processor(image, return_tensors="pt")\n\nwith torch.no_grad():\n    logits = model(**inputs).logits\n\n# model predicts one of the 1000 ImageNet classes\npredicted_label = logits.argmax(-1).item()\nprint(model.config.id2label[predicted_label])\n```\n\nFor more code examples, we refer to the (M): 25.6\n  - GMACs: 4.1\n  - Activations (M): 11.1\n  - Image size: 224 x 224\n- **Papers:**\n  - Billion-scale semi-supervised learning for image classification: https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.00546\n  - Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition: https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385\n- **Original:** https://github.com/facebookresearch/semi-supervised-ImageNet1K-models\n\n## Model Usage\n### Image Classification\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model('resnet50.fb_swsl_ig1b_ft_in1k', pretrained=True)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\ntop5_probabilities, top5_class_indices = torch.topk(output.softmax(dim=1) * 100, k=5)\n```\n\n### Feature Map Extraction\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50.fb_swsl_ig1b_ft_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    features_only=True,\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\nfor o in output:\n    # print shape of each feature map in output\n    # e.g.:\n    #  torch.Size([1, 64, 112, 112])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 256, 56, 56])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 512, 28, 28])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 1024, 14, 14])\n    #  torch.Size([1, 2048, 7, 7])\n\n    print(o.shape)\n```\n\n### Image Embeddings\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50.fb_swsl_ig1b_ft_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n    num_classes=0,  # remove classifier nn.Linear\n)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without needing to set num_classes=0)\n\noutput = model.forward_features(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))\n# output is unpooled, a (1, 2048, 7, 7) shaped tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm below.\n\nRecipe details:\n * RandAugment `RA2` recipe. Inspired by and evolved from EfficientNet RandAugment recipes. Published as `B` recipe in [ResNet Strikes Back](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.00476).\n * RMSProp (TF 1.0 behaviour) optimizer, EMA weight averaging\n * Step (exponential decay w/ staircase) LR schedule with warmup\n\n\n## Model Details\n- **Model Type:** Image classification / feature backbone\n- **Model Stats:**\n  - Params (M): 25.6\n  - GMACs: 4.4\n  - Activations (M): 11.9\n  - Image size: train = 224 x 224, test = 288 x 288\n- **Papers:**\n  - ResNet strikes back: An improved training procedure in timm: https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.00476\n  - Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition: https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385\n  - Bag of Tricks for Image Classification with Convolutional Neural Networks: https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.01187\n- **Original:** Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model('resnet50d.ra2_in1k', pretrained=True)\nmodel = model.eval()\n\n# get model specific transforms (normalization, resize)\ndata_config = timm.data.resolve_model_data_config(model)\ntransforms = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # unsqueeze single image into batch of 1\n\ntop5_probabilities, top5_class_indices = torch.topk(output.softmax(dim=1) * 100, k=5)\n```\n\n### Feature Map Extraction\n```python\nfrom urllib.request import urlopen\nfrom PIL import Image\nimport timm\n\nimg = Image.open(urlopen(\n    'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50d.ra2_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n 'https://huggingface.co/datasets/huggingface/documentation-images/resolve/main/beignets-task-guide.png'\n))\n\nmodel = timm.create_model(\n    'resnet50d.ra2_in1k',\n    pretrained=True,\n = timm.data.create_transform(**data_config, is_training=False)\n\noutput = model(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))  # output is (batch_size, num_features) shaped tensor\n\n# or equivalently (without needing to set num_classes=0)\n\noutput = model.forward_features(transforms(img).unsqueeze(0))\n# output is unpooled, a (1, 2048, 7, 7) shaped tensor\n\noutput = model.forward_head(output, pre_logits=True)\n# output is a (1, num_features) shaped tensor\n```\n\n## Model Comparison\nExplore the dataset and runtime metrics of this model in timm [model Dataset Details\n\n### Dataset Description\n\n<!-- Provide a longer summary of what this dataset is. -->\nThe CIFAR-10-C dataset is an extension of CIFAR-10 designed to evaluate model robustness to common corruptions. It consists of 950,000 images derived from the original CIFAR-10 test set (10,000 images) by applying 19 different corruption types at 5 severity levels. The corruptions include noise, blur, weather effects, and digital distortions. This dataset is widely used for benchmarking robustness in image classification tasks.\n\n### Dataset Sources\n\n<!-- Provide the basic links for the dataset. -->\n\n- **Homepage:** https://github.com/hendrycks/robustness\n- **Paper:** Hendrycks, D., & Dietterich, T.     "consistency_feedback": "Overall impression\nThe manuscript lays out a clear motivation, proposes a very small modification of TENT, and designs three well-targeted experiments that would, in principle, test accuracy, convergence speed, and robustness to domain reversals.  However, the evidence presented so far is largely *anticipated* rather than *observed*.  Because almost all numbers in the text are prefixed by words such as “≈” or are placed under “Expected Result / Expected Outcome”, the reader cannot verify that any run was actually executed.  Hence the central scientific claim – that ES-TENT achieves the same or better accuracy with ~40–60 % fewer optimisation steps – is not yet backed by empirical data.\n\n1. Experimental Strategy Issues\n   • Scope & metrics: The chosen datasets, models, baselines, and metrics are appropriate and mirror prior TTA literature.  Reporting AUC of error-vs-samples is a nice way to quantify convergence speed.\n   • Missing *real* results: Tables/figures are described but not shown.  Without the realised numbers, no statistical comparison (e.g. paired t-test of final error, confidence intervals for speed-ups) can be performed.\n   • Statistical power: Only 3 seeds are planned.  Detecting ≈0.3–0.6 pp differences in error may require more repetitions or a formal power analysis.\n\n2. Implementation Issues\n   • Equality test for skipping:  `if s.item()==0.0` will almost never be true once entropy is converted to FP32 because H̄ seldom lands *exactly* on `H_low`.  This could dramatically reduce the intended number of skipped steps and compromise the claimed 40–60 % reduction.  A robust implementation would use a tolerance (`<= 1e-6`) or compute the Boolean from the clipped value before casting.\n   • Optimiser state: When updates are skipped, the momentum buffer in SGD is left untouched.  This matches the verbal description, but it should be stated explicitly because stale momentum can later apply to the next non-skipped step and partly negate the “rest when confident” effect.\n\n3. Strategy-Implementation Alignment\n   • Loss scaling vs “larger step”: Multiplying the *loss* by `s` scales the *gradient*, not the learning rate.  The text claims this is “equivalent to multiplying the learning-rate by s”, which is true only for plain SGD without momentum or adaptive terms.  Because the experiments use SGD+momentum, the equivalence is approximate.  A short analytic derivation or an additional ablation (momentum = 0) would resolve this minor mismatch.\n\n4. Result Interpretation Issues\n   • No empirical variance: Improvements of 0.4 pp (CIFAR-10-C) and 0.5 pp (ImageNet-C) are within the run-to-run variance typically reported for TENT.  Significance cannot be judged without the actual mean ± sd numbers.\n   • Efficiency claim: A 30–35 % wall-clock speed-up is plausible, but depends heavily on implementation details such as AMP, dataloader overlap, and GPU utilisation when backward is skipped.  Again, real measurements are needed.\n   • Memory identical?: Skipping backward saves the activations needed for gradient computation, so peak memory should *decrease*, not remain “identical”.  Either revise the expectation or measure and report the true value.\n\nRecommendations (within scope)\n– Replace all “expected” numbers with the *observed* mean ± sd across seeds and give the exact number of skipped vs total batches.\n– Report p-values or confidence intervals for the main accuracy and efficiency improvements.\n– Fix the floating-point equality issue in the implementation and re-measure the percentage of skipped batches.\n– Clarify the momentum interaction and, if needed, show an ablation with/without momentum to confirm the explanation of ‘larger effective step’.",
